● I've read through the repo and drafted the Phase 4 checklist in
  docs/tasks/tasks4.md. That file is the only change I made. I didn't start any
  services or run anything that modifies the system.

  Where things stand

  - Gateway is done and already routes /llm/lookup and /llm/vision-infer to MS2
    (gateway/router.py).
  - MS1 works for the happy path. When the food isn't in the database, it falls
    back to a hardcoded calorie table and finally a default of 100 kcal
    (ms1_cv/main.py). MS2 will replace that.
  - MS2 and MS4 only have a health-check endpoint so far (ms2_llm/main.py).
  - Existing pieces MS2 can reuse:
    - shared/audit.py already logs to the audit_logs table.
    - The system_prompt_registry table exists with nutrition_lookup_v1 and
      vision_infer_v1 prompts. Those prompts don't ask for the strict JSON
      format the architecture specifies, so the plan adds v2 versions.

  Things that shaped the plan

  1. GPU: your card is an RTX 3050 with 6 GB of memory. The planned Qwen2.5-7B
     model plus YOLO won't fit together, and a local vision model on top of that
     certainly won't. So the plan uses qwen2.5:3b-instruct locally for text,
     with Gemini as the backup. For vision, Gemini goes first because it's the
     more reliable option, and the CV fallback needs to be reliable.
  2. Gemini key: GEMINI_API_KEY is already in .env, and the old MS3 code calls
     Gemini, so it costs us nothing to add.
  3. Ollama: it's installed in WSL but no models have been downloaded yet.
  4. Config bug: .env.example points OLLAMA_BASE_URL at ms2-llm:11434, but
     nothing in that container runs Ollama.
  5. MS3 is not "done": the master plan says it is, but ms3_user/main.py is just
     a health check. The real app sits unwired in ms3_user/NutriX-main/. Phase
     5 will be bigger than the plan suggests.
  6. Stray file: the deleted =0.0.9 in git status is leftover from an unquoted
     pip install python-multipart>=0.0.9. It's safe to leave it deleted.
  7. Databases: Postgres and Redis aren't running right now, so services quietly
     fall back to SQLite.

  What tasks4 covers

  - MS2 core: settings, request/response formats, a chain of model providers
    (local Ollama first, then Gemini) with a 10-second limit, and prompts loaded
    from the database.
  - Sanity checks on model output: values must be within sensible ranges, and
    the calories must roughly match the protein/carbs/fat (the standard 4/4/9
    kcal per gram rule).
  - Three endpoints:
    - lookup checks the database first and saves new foods it learns.
    - vision-infer returns the top 3 guesses, with macros taken from the
      database where possible.
    - generate is an internal endpoint for MS4.
  - MS1 wiring: this completes tasks3 items 3.5 and 3.6, which is the CV
    fallback you asked for.
  - Packaging: Docker setup with an Ollama container, tests that run in CI
    without any model or database, and doc updates.

  Decisions I need from you

  1. Meals the user hasn't confirmed yet. When YOLO isn't confident, should MS1
     log the top vision guess straight away, marked as unconfirmed so a later
     confirmation can fix it? That's my recommendation, because the flow then
     works end to end without building /hitl/confirm now. Or should nothing be
     logged until the user confirms?
  2. Sending images to Gemini. Is it OK for scale photos to go to a cloud API?
  3. Model size. Should we stay on the 3B model, or will a machine with a bigger
     GPU host MS2 later?

  Commands to run once you're ready (in WSL)

  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && docker compose up -d postgres redis
  ollama pull qwen2.5:3b-instruct
  # optional local vision model:
  ollama pull qwen2.5vl:3b

  Once you've answered the questions, I'll finalise tasks4 and start on 4.1
  (settings and request/response formats).

  import os
  import time
  import logging
  import gc
  from typing import Dict, Any, Optional

  import torch
  from PIL import Image

  try:
      from transformers import AutoModelForImageTextToText, AutoProcessor
      from qwen_vl_utils import process_vision_info
  except ImportError:
      AutoModelForImageTextToText = None
      AutoProcessor = None
      process_vision_info = None

  from .nlp_config import config

  logger = logging.getLogger("nlp_engine.vlm")


  class VLMEngine:
      """
      On-Demand Vision-Language Model Engine (Qwen3-VL-2B).
      Loads model weights into VRAM only when queried, runs inference, and
  frees memory immediately.
      """

      DEFAULT_SNAPSHOT_PATH = r"C:\Users\HP\.cache\huggingface\hub\models--Qwen
  --Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct\snapshots\89644892e4d85e24eaac8bacfd4f463576704203"

      def __init__(self, model_id: Optional[str] = None):
          # Prefer the local cached snapshot if available
          if model_id is None:
              if os.path.isdir(self.DEFAULT_SNAPSHOT_PATH):
                  self.model_id = self.DEFAULT_SNAPSHOT_PATH
              else:
                  self.model_id = getattr(config, "vlm_model",
  "Qwen/Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct")
          else:
              self.model_id = model_id

          self.device = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
          self.dtype = (
              torch.bfloat16
              if (self.device == "cuda" and torch.cuda.is_bf16_supported())
              else (torch.float16 if self.device == "cuda" else torch.float32)
          )

          self.flag_path = "frame_request.flag"
          self.frame_path = "latest_frame.jpg"
          self.is_busy = False

          logger.info(f"VLMEngine initialized with model: {self.model_id} on
  {self.device} ({self.dtype})")

      def _request_frame(self, timeout: float = 2.0) -> Optional[str]:
          """
          Request the CV pipeline to capture and save the latest frame via Pull
  Architecture.
          """
          try:
              if os.path.exists(self.flag_path):
                  try:
                      os.remove(self.flag_path)
                  except OSError:
                      pass

              # Signal CV pipeline
              with open(self.flag_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
                  f.write("request")

              deadline = time.time() + timeout
              while time.time() < deadline:
                  # When pipeline writes latest_frame.jpg, it re
  ──── (174 lines hidden) ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ds_trimmed,
                  skip_special_tokens=True,
                  clean_up_tokenization_spaces=False,
              )[0].strip()

              logger.info(f"VLM responded in {inf_duration:.2f}s:
  '{output_text}'")
              return output_text

          except Exception as e:
              logger.error(f"Error during VLM execution: {e}", exc_info=True)
              if language == "hi":
                  return "क्षमा करें, मुझे इस दृश्य का विश्लेषण करने में समस्या आ रही है।"
              elif language == "gu":
                  return "માફ કરશો, આ દ્રશ્ય સમજવામાં સમસ્યા આવી રહી છે."
              return "Sorry, I had trouble analyzing the scene."

          finally:
              self.is_busy = False
              # Enforce immediate memory release to return to steady-state VRAM
              if model is not None:
                  del model
              if processor is not None:
                  del processor
              gc.collect()
              if torch.cuda.is_available():
                  torch.cuda.empty_cache()
              logger.info("Qwen3-VL-2B unloaded. VRAM freed.")


  # Global instance
  vlm_engine = VLMEngine()

  Lifecycle of the Spike:Steady-State Baseline (Before Query): Only the CV
  stack occupies the GPU at 0.46 GB (467.87 MB). STT (faster-whisper INT8) and
  offline TTS (pyttsx3) run entirely on the CPU (0.00 MB VRAM).   On-Demand
  Allocation: When triggered via push-to-talk, weights are pulled into GPU
  memory (3.96 GB) in 7,051.77 ms (7.05 s).   Simultaneous Peak Spike: During
  the 1,535.53 ms token generation phase, the system reaches its maximum
  concurrency of 4.53 GB (4,636.29 MB / 75.5% utilization).   Post-Query
  Eviction: Weights and generation caches are immediately purged via garbage
  collection and torch.cuda.empty_cache(), dropping residual VRAM back down to
  15.17 MB above baseline and returning the GPU to steady-state CV execution.

● Yes, it's feasible. I checked the WSL side directly:

  - The model is reachable. Qwen3-VL-2B (4.0 GB, model.safetensors) is in your
    Windows cache, and WSL can read it at
    /mnt/c/Users/HP/.cache/huggingface/hub/models--Qwen--Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct.
  - The GPU works from WSL. The .venv sees CUDA, and the card supports bf16, so
    the same precision as your sample code applies.
  - Packages are missing. transformers, accelerate and qwen-vl-utils aren't
    installed in the WSL .venv yet.

  Adjustments to your numbers for NutriX

  - Peak memory will be a bit higher. In your other project, YOLO and Qwen run
    in one process. Here MS1 (YOLO) and MS2 (Qwen) are separate processes, and
    each one reserves roughly 0.3–0.4 GB of GPU memory just for setup. I'd
    expect a peak of about 5.0–5.2 GB out of 6 GB. That still fits, but the
    safety margin drops to about 1 GB.
  - Loading on every request is too slow. Your measured cost is a 7 s load plus
    1.5 s of generation, about 8.6 s per call. The architecture's 10 s limit
    leaves almost no slack. Loading from /mnt/c is also slower than from the WSL
    filesystem, so it could break that limit.
  - No frame-request flag file. MS1 will send the image bytes to MS2 over HTTP
    instead of using the frame_request.flag handshake from your sample.
    Everything else in your engine carries over: the chat template,
    process_vision_info, and the cleanup after each call.

  Remaining questions

  1. When should MS2 be called? ms1_cv/main.py starts the CV engine with
     confidence_threshold=0.3, but the architecture says 80%. Which value counts
     as "unidentified"?
     - My recommendation: keep 0.3 for logging and send anything below it to
       Qwen.
  2. What if Qwen's label isn't in the database? Your flow says Qwen names the
     food and the rest works like YOLO. If the database lookup then misses, what
     should happen?
     - My recommendation: in the same Qwen call, ask for the label plus an
       estimated calories, protein, carbs and fat per 100 g. Use the estimate
       only if the database misses, and save it to foods marked as LLM-inferred
       so the same food is a database hit next time. A 2B model's macro
       estimates will be rough, so the alternative is a placeholder value.
  3. Keep the model loaded or load it per call?
     - My recommendation: keep it loaded for a few minutes after each use, then
       unload it. Steady usage would be about 4.5–5 GB while it's loaded, and
       repeated calls would skip the 7 s load.
     - Either way, I'd raise the MS1→MS2 timeout from 10 s to about 30 s. The
       gateway already allows 60 s for weight-frame uploads.
  4. Should Qwen pick from a fixed list or name the food freely?
     - My recommendation: let it answer freely, pass YOLO's low-confidence guess
       as a hint, then match its answer against the database's exact names,
       aliases and partial matches. Limiting it to YOLO's 123 classes would
       defeat the point of a fallback.
  5. Where should the model files live? I'd copy them into the WSL Hugging Face
     cache for faster loading, and point to the folder with an env var
     (VLM_MODEL_PATH) rather than a hardcoded path. You'd run:
  mkdir -p ~/.cache/huggingface/hub && cp -r
  /mnt/c/Users/HP/.cache/huggingface/hub/models--Qwen--Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct
  ~/.cache/huggingface/hub/

  Once you answer these, or just say "go with your recommendations", I'll
  rewrite tasks4.md around local Qwen3-VL-2B, drop Gemini, Ollama and HitL, and
  wait for your confirmation before writing any code.

● Here are the answers to the two things you asked me to check (food coverage
  and model location), plus a small open point on the lassi/kulfi fix.

  Q4: how many foods do we have macros for?

  Postgres is down, so I counted the rows in the files the seeders load:

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │                 Source                 │             Foods             │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ wholeFoods.xlsx (the 123 YOLO classes) │ 123                           │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Indian_Food_DF.csv                     │ 1,460                         │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ UK_fct.xlsx                            │ 144                           │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ indian_food_nutrition_dataset.csv      │ 97                            │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ US_fct.xlsx                            │ 54                            │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Total                                  │ about 1,880, plus 207 aliases │
  └────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  That's an estimate before duplicates are removed. Once Postgres is up, SELECT
  count(*) FROM foods; gives the real number.

  My recommendation is to let Qwen name the food freely, then match its answer
  against the database.
  - Restricting it to the 123 YOLO classes would make the fallback pointless,
    since the misses are exactly what we need it for.
  - Putting all ~1,880 names in the prompt would add over 10k tokens per call.
    That's slow on a 2B model and would raise the memory spike during
    generation.
  - With free naming, Qwen's answer goes through the existing exact → alias →
    partial match. If that misses, Qwen's own macro estimate is used and saved,
    as you agreed in Q2.

  Q5: is it worth moving the model into WSL? No, skip it.

  - Read speed: WSL reads the model from /mnt/c at about 214 MB/s, so a cold
    load takes about 20 s.
  - Load once: since we're keeping the model loaded, MS2 will load it once at
    startup, not per request. That makes the 20 s a one-time cost.
  - No real storage saving: the WSL filesystem is itself a virtual disk file
    stored on C:. Copying the model in would still take 4 GB of your 91 GB.
  - One catch: with uvicorn --reload, every code edit restarts MS2 and reloads
    the model (about 20 s each time). I'll document running MS2 without
    --reload.

  Lassi/kulfi fix

  - Where: in ms1_cv/cv_engine.py, when the top detection is lassi or kulfi, it
    becomes unidentified and the frame goes to Qwen.
  - No hint: YOLO's guess won't be passed to Qwen for these two, so it can't
    steer Qwen toward the same wrong answer.
  - Configurable: I'll make the list an env var
    (YOLO_SUPPRESSED_CLASSES=lassi,kulfi) so other troublesome classes can be
    added without code changes.
  - Side effect: real lassi and kulfi will always go through Qwen too, adding a
    couple of seconds for those.
  - Open point: should I instead check whether YOLO's next-best detection in the
    same frame is a non-suppressed class above 0.3, and use that before calling
    Qwen? I'd keep it strict, as you described, unless you want this.

  Plan going forward

  - Model: Qwen3-VL-2B loads once at MS2 startup from your Windows cache path,
    set via the VLM_MODEL_PATH env var. No Gemini or Ollama.
  - One Qwen call per frame: it returns the food label plus estimated calories,
    protein, carbs and fat per 100 g.
  - Lookup order: database first; Qwen's estimate only on a miss, saved as
    LLM-inferred.
  - Timeout: MS1→MS2 goes up to 30 s.
  - HitL: skipped. If YOLO identifies the food, MS1 logs it; if not, Qwen's
    answer goes through the same database lookup and logging.
  - What I'll measure: steady and peak GPU memory with MS1 and MS2 both loaded,
    to settle whether keeping the model resident works.

  Let me know about the open point and whether free naming works for you. On
  your confirmation I'll rewrite tasks4.md to match, then start coding.

> open point:
  for passing next best detection, we will need to test yolo again. So for now,
  lets skip that. Lets work on qwen as fallback.
  So for now, rewrite task4 and wait for my confirmation before implementing.

          +e C: disk |                                                   
       31 +| **Residency** | Load **once at MS2 startup** and keep it res
          +ident. Measure VRAM with MS1 + MS2 both up. If it's too tight,
          + switch to on-demand load/unload (the pattern from the referen
          +ce `VLMEngine`) behind a config flag |                        
       32 +| **One call per frame** | Qwen returns the food label **and**
          + per-100 g macros (kcal, protein, carbs, fat) in one JSON resp
          +onse |                                                        
       33 +| **Macro source of truth** | DB first. Qwen macros are used o
          +nly on a DB miss, then persisted (`brand="LLM Inferred"`, `foo
          +d_code="LLM_<slug>"`) so the next lookup hits the DB |        
       34 +| **Label space** | Free-form food name, with no fixed class l
          +ist in the prompt. The DB has ~1,880 foods (123 wholeFoods + 1
          +,460 Indian_Food_DF + 144 UK + 97 Indian nutrition + 54 US, pr
          +e-dedup); listing them would add >10k tokens per call |       
       35 +| **YOLO hint** | YOLO's low-confidence guess is passed to Qwe
          +n as a hint, **except** for suppressed classes (lassi/kulfi), 
          +so it can't bias Qwen toward the same mistake |               
       36 +| **Lassi/kulfi fix** | If YOLO's top detection is lassi or ku
          +lfi, treat the frame as unidentified. The list is configurable
          + via `YOLO_SUPPRESSED_CLASSES=lassi,kulfi`. No next-best-box f
          +allback for now (that needs YOLO re-testing) |                
       37 +| **HitL** | Skipped this phase. No `/hitl/confirm`, no confir
          +mation step. Qwen's answer is logged directly |               
       38 +| **Timeouts** | MS1 → MS2 timeout of **30 s**. The gateway al
          +ready allows 60 s for `weight-frame` |                        
       39 +| **YOLO threshold** | Stays at **0.3** (testing value) |     
       40 +

● I've rewritten docs/tasks/tasks4.md around Qwen3-VL-2B as the fallback. I
  haven't touched any code.

  Changes from the previous draft
  - Model: local Qwen3-VL-2B only, loaded with Hugging Face transformers like
    your sample. Gemini and Ollama are gone.
  - Model files: they stay in your Windows cache, and the path is set with
    VLM_MODEL_PATH. The model loads once when MS2 starts, about 20 s from
    /mnt/c.
  - Keeping it loaded: it stays in GPU memory for now. 4.19 measures memory with
    MS1 and MS2 both running, and VLM_KEEP_LOADED=false switches back to your
    load-and-unload-per-call approach if it's too tight.
  - Qwen's answer: one call per frame returns the food name plus per-100 g
    calories, protein, carbs and fat. The database is checked first, and Qwen's
    numbers are only used and saved when the food isn't found there.
  - Lassi/kulfi: these are treated as unidentified and sent to Qwen without
    YOLO's guess as a hint. The list is configurable through
    YOLO_SUPPRESSED_CLASSES.
  - Confirmation step and next-best detection: both skipped for now and listed
    under out of scope.
  - Timeout: MS1 waits up to 30 s for MS2.
  - Hardcoded fallback: MS1's built-in calorie table and its default of 100 kcal
    are replaced by MS2's /llm/lookup.

  qwen-vl-utils

       1 """
       2 Shared food lookup used by MS1 (scale ingestion) and MS2 (LLM nu

● The MS2 service and the MS1 fallback are written, but none of it has been run.
  The checks I did were syntax parsing and one import of ms2_llm.prompts. The
  tests, the model load, VRAM and the end-to-end flow still need you to run
  them, per your rule.

  What was built

  - MS2 endpoints (ms2_llm/main.py): both are new.
    - POST /api/v1/llm/vision-infer takes the image, the weight and an optional
      YOLO hint. It returns Qwen's food label plus per-100 g macros.
    - POST /api/v1/llm/lookup checks the database first and falls back to Qwen
      on a miss, then saves the new food.
    - Both write an audit log entry in the background.
    - /health now reports the model's load state and GPU memory.
  - Qwen engine (ms2_llm/vlm_engine.py): ported from your sample.
    - It loads once at startup and stays resident.
    - With VLM_KEEP_LOADED=false it loads per call and frees memory after, as in
      your sample.
    - Image bytes arrive over HTTP, with no flag files.
  - Output checks (ms2_llm/validation.py): reply parsing and sanity checks.
    - It strips fences, parses the JSON and normalises the label.
    - It flags impossible macros (over 900 kcal, macros over 100 g, or calories
      that don't match the macros).
    - Unparseable output is retried once, then returned as unknown.
  - Shared lookup (shared/food_lookup.py): exact name, then alias, then partial
    match, as MS1 had it. LIKE wildcards are now escaped. LLM-inferred foods are
    saved with brand "LLM Inferred".
  - MS1 (ms1_cv/main.py, ms1_cv/cv_engine.py, ms1_cv/llm_client.py):
    - Lassi/kulfi: now count as unidentified, and YOLO's guess isn't passed on
      as a hint. The list comes from YOLO_SUPPRESSED_CLASSES.
    - Fallback: unidentified frames go to MS2 with a 30 s timeout. The label
      goes through the database lookup. Qwen's macros are used only on a miss,
      then saved.
    - Response and log: the response now includes identified_by and
      macro_source. meal_logs.source is esp32_yolo, esp32_qwen_vlm or
      llm-timeout.

  Things you might not expect

  - No more made-up calories: the hardcoded calorie table and the 100 kcal
    default are gone. If both the database and MS2 fail, the meal is logged with
    0 kcal and macro_source: "llm-timeout".
  - Split requirements: the heavy packages (transformers, accelerate,
    qwen-vl-utils) are in ms2_llm/requirements-vlm.txt so CI doesn't download
    multi-GB wheels. They're imported lazily.
  - Env files: I replaced the Ollama block in .env and .env.example with the VLM
    settings, including VLM_MODEL_PATH pointing at your Windows cache.
  - Compose: the ms2-llm service now mounts your cache read-only and has a GPU
    reservation. I didn't test Docker.
  - Seeder: seed_prompts.py now adds the two v2 prompts. It overwrites existing
    prompt text on re-seed, as it already did.
  - Docker database URL: shared/db.py rewrites @postgres: to localhost, which
    will break inside containers. That predates my changes, and I didn't touch
    it.

  Commands for you to run (in WSL)

  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate

  # 1. Unit tests (no GPU, model or Postgres needed)
  PYTHONPATH=. pytest ms2_llm/tests ms1_cv/tests -v

  # 2. Re-seed prompts so the v2 prompts are in the database
  PYTHONPATH=. python shared/seeders/seed_prompts.py

  # 3. Start MS2 (no --reload; the first load takes about 20 s)
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn ms2_llm.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8003
  # in another terminal:
  curl -s localhost:8003/health

  # 4. Direct MS2 checks
  PYTHONPATH=. python scripts/test_ms2.py --image
  dataset/live_captures/latest_raw.jpg --weight 180
  PYTHONPATH=. python scripts/test_ms2.py --lookup "paneer tikka" --lookup
  "murgh makhani"

  # 5. Full path: start MS1 with the MS2 server still running, then send a
  lassi/kulfi frame
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn ms1_cv.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8001
  PYTHONPATH=. python scripts/test_ms2.py --image <lassi_frame.jpg> --weight 200
  --via-ms1
  For the VRAM measurement (task 4.19), run nvidia-smi while MS1 and MS2 are
  both up.

  Still open in tasks4.md

  - 4.0.4: count of foods in the database.
  - 4.17 to 4.19: running the tests, the integration script and the VRAM
    measurement.
  - 4.20: ticking Phase 4 in the master plan, once the checks above pass.

  Send me any test failures or the output of test_ms2.py and I'll fix them.

      148  
      149 +### GPU memory (measured on the RTX 3050 6 GB)                
      150 +| State | VRAM |                                              
      151 +|---|---|                                                     
      152 +| MS2 model resident (allocated by PyTorch) | ~4,058 MB (peak 
          +~4,116 MB during inference) |                                 
      153 +| `nvidia-smi` total with MS2 up | ~4,274–4,368 MiB (≈ 71 %) |
      154 +| Inference latency | 2.5–4.5 s (first call includes warm-up) 
          +|                                                             
      155 +| Cold load from `/mnt/c` | ~32 s |                           
      156 +                                                              
      157 +Watch the combined footprint with MS1 running too:            
      158 +```bash                                                       
      159 +watch -n 1 nvidia-smi --query-gpu=memory.used,memory.total --f
          +ormat=csv,noheader                                            
      160 +```                                                           
      161 +If you hit CUDA out-of-memory, set `VLM_KEEP_LOADED=false` in 
          +`.env` and restart MS2 (loads per call, slower but frees VRAM)
          +.

      131 -#### How It Works:                                            
      132 -1. The script resets today's test logs in DB and Redis so you 
          -start with `0.0 kcal`.                                        
      133 -2. A window titled **"NutriX Dev Capture"** appears showing yo
          -ur live webcam.                                               
      134 -3. Hold up a food item (e.g., Apple, Banana, Bread, Tomato).  
      135 -4. Press **`SPACE`** to freeze the frame and capture.         
      136 -5. In your terminal, you will see:                            
      137 -   ```text                                                    
      138 -   >>> Enter food weight in grams [default: 150.0g]:          
      139 -   ```                                                        
      140 -   Type the weight (e.g. `125`) and press Enter, or simply pre
          -ss Enter to accept `150.0g`.                                  
      141 -6. The script sends the multipart request to MS1, prints the r
          -esult in terminal, and opens the annotated detection in your b
          -rowser at `http://localhost:8001/captures/annotated`.         
      142 -7. You can continue capturing more foods, or press **`Q`** to 
          -exit.                                                         
      191 +# JWT routes (MS2 text lookup via the gateway)                
      192 +JWT=$(PYTHONPATH=. python -c "from gateway.generate_token impo
          +rt get_test_token; print(get_test_token())")                  
      193 +curl -s -X POST localhost:8000/api/v1/llm/lookup -H "Authoriza
          +tion: Bearer $JWT" \                                          
      194 +  -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"food_name":"paneer
          + tikka"}'                                                     
      195 +curl -s -o /dev/null -w "llm/lookup without JWT -> %{http_code
          +}\n" -X POST localhost:8000/api/v1/llm/lookup \               
      196 +  -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"food_name":"x"}'  
      197  
      198 +# Unknown route -> 404; stopped service -> 502                
      199 +curl -s localhost:8000/api/v1/nope                            
      200 +```                                                           
      201 +Expected: `401`/`403` for missing or bad credentials, `404` fo
          +r unknown routes, `502` if the target service is down, `504` o
          +n timeout.

      244 -## 8. Summary Checklist for Daily Development                 
      291 +## 9. Troubleshooting                                         
      292  
      293 +| Symptom | Cause | Fix |                                     
      294 +|---|---|---|                                                 
      295 +| `macro_source: llm-timeout`, 0 kcal | MS2 not running, still
          + loading, or `MS2_LLM_URL` wrong | `curl localhost:8003/health
          +`; wait for `vlm.status: ok` |                                
      296 +| MS2 `/health` says `degraded`, `vlm.status: not_loaded` | Mo
          +del failed to load (see `vlm.error`) | Check `VLM_MODEL_PATH`;
          + `pip install` the VLM deps; read the MS2 terminal |          
      297 +| CUDA out of memory | MS1 + MS2 + other GPU apps exceed 6 GB 
          +| Close other GPU apps, or `VLM_KEEP_LOADED=false` |          
      298 +| Vision result `flagged: true`, `calories` inconsistent | 2B 
          +model's kcal doesn't match its macros (e.g. idli 240 kcal vs 3
          +.5/32/2 g) | Expected occasionally. MS1 ignores flagged macros
          + and uses the DB entry or `/llm/lookup` |                     
      299 +| Same wrong label every time for lassi/kulfi | Class not in s
          +uppression list | Add it to `YOLO_SUPPRESSED_CLASSES` in `.env
          +` and restart MS1 |                                           
      300 +| Gateway `502 Bad Gateway` | Target service not running | Sta
          +rt it; check the URL log line at gateway startup |            
      301 +| Gateway `504` | Service exceeded route timeout (weight-frame
          +: 60 s) | Check MS2 isn't stuck; worst-case Qwen path is ~35 s
          + |                                                            
      302 +| First MS2 request slow | Normal warm-up | Send one throwaway
          + request after start |                                        
      303 +| Negative / huge Redis totals | Stale test data | `scripts/re
          +set_test_session.py` |                                        
      304 +                                                              
      305 +---

          + 40 s); the default of many Arduino HTTP clients is 5 s, which

          +l logged correctly.

● The two runbooks are updated and the command list is below. Nothing was run on
  my side. I did make three small script edits: dev_capture.py now prints
  identified_by and macro_source (and waits 60 s instead of 30), and
  show_db_logs.py gained a Source column.

  Your test results

  - MS2 standalone works: the model loaded in 32 s and takes about 4.1 GB of GPU
    memory. Inference took 2.5–4.5 s. nvidia-smi showed 71 % usage with MS2
    alone, so there should be headroom for YOLO. I haven't seen MS1 and MS2
    measured together, so step 3 below watches that.
  - The idli frame was flagged: Qwen gave 240 kcal but macros that add up to
    about 160. Here MS1 would use the database's idli entry, so it's harmless.
    For a food that isn't in the database, a flagged answer ends up logged as 0
    kcal.
    - I'd suggest recomputing calories from the macros when that's the only
      problem, instead of discarding the whole answer. Do you want that?
  - The lookup test didn't reach Qwen: paneer tikka and banana are both already
    in the database. To test the Qwen text path, use a food that isn't there,
    such as murgh makhani, and run it twice. The first run should say
    qwen3-vl-2b and the second db.
  - No pytest output: I haven't seen the unit test results yet, so please send
    them.

  Commands to run (in WSL)

  Start MS2 first, then MS1, then the gateway, one terminal each.

  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate

  # 0. Setup and unit tests
  docker compose up -d postgres redis
  PYTHONPATH=. python shared/seeders/seed_prompts.py
  PYTHONPATH=. pytest ms2_llm/tests ms1_cv/tests gateway/tests -v
  PYTHONPATH=. python scripts/reset_test_session.py

  # 1. Terminal 1: MS2 (no --reload)
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn ms2_llm.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8003
  # 2. Terminal 2: MS1
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn ms1_cv.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8001 --reload
  # 3. Terminal 3: gateway
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn gateway.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8000 --reload
  # 4. Optional: MS3 and MS4 (health only)
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn ms3_user.main:app --port 8002
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn ms4_agents.main:app --port 8004

  print(d['service'], d['status'])" || echo DOWN; done

  # A. YOLO-known food, direct to MS1

  $JWT" -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"food_name":"paneer tikka"}'

  PYTHONPATH=. python scripts/show_redis_cache.py
  docker exec nutrix-postgres psql -U nutrix -d nutrix_db -c "SELECT source,
  count(*) FROM meal_logs GROUP BY source;"
  docker exec nutrix-postgres psql -U nutrix -d nutrix_db -c "SELECT service,
  operation, flagged, latency_ms FROM audit_logs ORDER BY id DESC LIMIT 5;"

  The docs also cover the bad-token checks, the LLM-added foods query and
  troubleshooting. They're in PIPELINE_2_SOFTWARE.md
  (docs/testing/PIPELINE_2_SOFTWARE.md) and PIPELINE_1_HARDWARE.md
  (docs/testing/PIPELINE_1_HARDWARE.md). One hardware note in Pipeline 1: the
  fallback path takes 3–6 s, so check the ESP32's HTTP timeout is at least 15 s,
  because many Arduino defaults are 5 s.

  Request tracker

  Yes, it's possible. The production-standard version is distributed tracing,
  where one trace ID follows a request through every service and each step shows
  its timing.

  There's a bug to fix first. The gateway makes up a request ID for its own log
  and sends a different one downstream (gateway/middleware/logging_mw.py versus
  gateway/proxy.py), and MS1 and MS2 don't log request IDs at all. So the logs
  can't be joined up today.

  Option: A. Lightweight, built in-house (my recommendation now)
  What you get: One request ID forwarded through gateway, MS1 and MS2. Each hop
  writes a timed event into a Redis stream. scripts/trace_tail.py shows them
  live in your terminal, grouped per request: gateway → ms1 YOLO 84 ms → ms2
  vision 3.1 s → DB hit → meal_log #12 → redis. It also works across machines
  later, since they share Redis.
  Cost: About half a day, no new dependencies
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Option: B. OpenTelemetry
  What you get: The industry standard: automatic tracing of FastAPI and httpx
  calls with timing charts, viewable in a Jaeger web UI or a terminal viewer
  such as otel-tui.
  Cost: Extra packages, a collector container and more setup

  I'd do A now and move to B in Phase 10, since A's event format can map onto B
  later. Want me to build A, and should I include the calorie-repair change from
  above?

  How does it look

● It looks good. The whole chain works end to end, and the failure case degrades
  the way it should.

  What your output confirms

  - YOLO path: tomato, idli, apple and bell pepper were identified by YOLO from
    the database in about 100–220 ms. The first tomato took 2.5 s, which I'd put
    down to model warm-up.
  - Lassi suppression: the banana photo that YOLO called lassi at 28.6 % went to
    Qwen, which correctly said banana. The Suppressed class reason shows in the
    output.
  - Qwen fallback: "No objects detected" on the sweet potato photo became Sweet
    Potato at 129 kcal from the database, in about 3 s.
  - MS2 down: the request returned 200 in 135 ms. It logged llm-timeout with 0
    kcal and didn't make up numbers.
  - Gateway: the device-token route works, and the weight-frame requests are
    logged with the target service.
  - Database and Redis: the Source column and audit rows show up. Redis's 773.1
    kcal matches the sum of the 8 meals.
  - GPU memory: about 4.5 GB of 6.1 GB (73 %) with MS1 and MS2 both loaded, so
    about 1.6 GB of headroom. Keeping Qwen resident is viable. I'm basing that
    on the 4,502 MiB reading in your test output. The watch view you started
    wasn't pasted.

  Things I'd act on

  1. Frames with a near-zero or negative weight still get logged. Your old rows
     (Unknown -2942 g, from uncalibrated scale readings) show it. With the
     current code such a frame is skipped by the CV engine, then logged as an
     Unknown meal with a junk weight, labelled esp32_yolo. It should be rejected
     with no log row. Small fix.
  2. YOLO answers at 38–53 % confidence are accepted without checking. Bell
     pepper at 38.5 %, apple at 42.9 % and idli at 52.8 % all skipped Qwen. The
     0.3 threshold is hardcoded in MS1. Now that the fallback exists, you could
     test a higher value such as 0.5. I'd make it an env var so it's easy to
     try.
  3. Qwen's estimates become permanent database entries.
     - Example: murgh makhani came back as 350 kcal per 100 g, which looks high
       to me.
     - Why it matters: it's now saved and will be treated as a database hit from
       now on.
     - Safeguard: these rows are tagged LLM Inferred, so they can be reviewed or
       deleted in bulk with the query in the runbook. Saving a food only after
       it's seen twice would be a safer design.
  4. Qwen's confidence is always 0.95–0.97. It carries no information, so don't
     build thresholds on it.
  5. About 1 in 4 vision calls was flagged: the macros didn't match the
     calories. The database covered it here, but for new foods it means 0 kcal.
     That's the calorie-repair change I mentioned earlier.
  6. 0-kcal Unknown rows from MS2 outages count as meals in Redis, so the day's
     meal count goes up even though no food was logged.

  Still open

  - The pytest output hasn't been shared yet. I'll tick Phase 4 in the master
    plan once it passes.
  - I haven't seen the bad-token gateway checks, and MS3 and MS4 were simply not
    started, which is fine.

  Want me to do items 1, 2 and 5 (reject empty-weight frames, a
  YOLO_CONF_THRESHOLD env var, and recomputing calories from macros) and then
  start on the request tracker?

  How the tracker would look

  What it does. Every request gets one ID at the gateway, and each service
  writes timed events under that ID into a Redis stream, which a terminal viewer
  displays live. This also fixes the gateway bug where the ID it logs differs
  from the one it forwards. Redis is already running, so there's nothing new to
  install.

  Live view (PYTHONPATH=. python scripts/trace_tail.py). The numbers below are
  made up for illustration:
  19:52:45  9098e0ad  POST /ingest/weight-frame  user=1                     ✔
  200   2,628 ms
    ├─ gateway     auth device-token ok → ms1
         2 ms
    ├─ ms1         YOLO  lassi 28.6% → suppressed
        94 ms
    ├─ ms1 → ms2   vision-infer
     2,410 ms
    │   └─ ms2     Qwen3-VL  "banana"  flagged=no
     2,390 ms
    ├─ ms1         DB lookup "banana"  hit (exact)  89 kcal/100g
         4 ms
    ├─ ms1         INSERT meal_logs #146  160.2 kcal  src=esp32_qwen_vlm
         6 ms
    └─ ms1         Redis sync  user:1  total 192.6 kcal
         3 ms

  19:53:49  02bb2bfa  POST /ingest/weight-frame  user=1                     ✔
  200      95 ms
    ├─ ms1         YOLO  idli 52.8% → accepted
        88 ms
    ...
  19:58:12  6106e884  POST /ingest/weight-frame  user=1                     ✔
  200     135 ms
    ├─ ms1 → ms2   vision-infer   ✖ ConnectError (MS2 down)
         3 ms
    └─ ms1         INSERT meal_logs #150  0 kcal  src=llm-timeout
  Failures show in red and flagged Qwen results in yellow. The viewer would also
  take options like --request 9098e0ad, --errors and --slow 1000.

  Building blocks
  1. Request ID: the gateway creates it once, forwards it, and returns it in the
     response header. MS1 passes it on to MS2, and dev_capture.py prints it.
  2. Events: a small shared/trace.py helper adds one event per step. It never
     blocks a request or raises an error, and it does nothing if Redis is down.
     It records labels and numbers only, never images.
  3. Steps recorded:
     - gateway: auth and routing
     - MS1: YOLO result, suppression, the MS2 call, DB lookup tier, meal log
       insert, Redis sync
     - MS2: image decode, Qwen generation, parse and validation, DB lookup,
       persist
  4. Retention: the stream is capped at about 5,000 events.
  5. Viewer: uses the rich library, which is already installed in your venv.

  Limits. This is a development tool, not full production monitoring. The event
  format maps onto OpenTelemetry (request ID ≈ trace ID, step ≈ span), so it can
  move to that in Phase 10.

  Questions before I build
  1. Is a Redis stream OK, rather than log files? I recommend the stream because
     it keeps working when services run on separate machines in Phase 9.
  2. Should it cover only the gateway, MS1 and MS2 for now? MS3 and MS4 have no
     real endpoints yet.
  3. Do you want dev_capture.py to print the request ID and the viewer to take
     --request <id>? I'd say yes.

● I've written docs/tasks/tasks5.md. It's a plan only: I haven't touched any
  code, run any commands, or moved any files.

  How MS3 will stitch in

  MS3 becomes a proper ms3_user package that uses our shared database models,
  the gateway and MS2. It reuses the same /api/v1/... paths the gateway already
  exposes, so the app gets one stable contract. Beyond the target diagram and
  the tasks, the file includes a route-by-route map from MS3's old paths to the
  new ones.

  Problems the audit found

  1. Almost no auth: only 6 of 58 routes require a logged-in user. The other 51
     are open and take a user_id in the path or body, so any caller could read
     or write another user's data.
  2. Token mismatch: MS3 puts the email in the JWT's sub claim, while our
     gateway expects the user ID there. The fix is to make sub the user ID.
  3. Two Redis writers clash: MS1 and the Postgres trigger write different
     shapes to the same daily-macros key, and deleting a meal never resyncs.
     This breaks the live dashboard once MS3 is running.
  4. Database clash: MS3 brings its own models, silently falls back to SQLite,
     and its alias column is called alias where ours is alias_name. Four new
     tables and four new columns need an Alembic migration.
  5. Seeders are broken: they still read the old MS3 folder, which was deleted.
     Re-running them now silently seeds nothing.
  6. User ID collisions: MS1 auto-creates users with fixed IDs, which will
     collide with real registrations.
  7. Gemini calls: MS3 calls Gemini directly in 7 or more places (AI coach, diet
     plan, OCR, barcode estimates), which conflicts with our decision to stay
     local. MS2 needs a new llm/generate endpoint first.
  8. Missing packages: your venv lacks ortools, xgboost, scikit-learn,
     rapidfuzz, bcrypt and pyjwt.

  Suggested order

  Each step ends with something you can run:
  1. MS3 boots on Postgres.
  2. Register and log in through the gateway.
  3. Scan food with dev_capture and see it on the dashboard.
  4. Seed recipes, fast food and regional data.
  5. Run the AI features through MS2.
  6. Add tests and the app contract.

  Questions for you

  My recommendation is first in each, and the plan assumes it until you say
  otherwise.
  1. Barcode and OCR: keep them in MS3, where the code and 20 tests already are,
     and re-route the gateway to MS3, or port them into MS1?
  2. AI features: run them on local Qwen through MS2 (they'll be weaker than
     Gemini), or keep an optional Gemini fallback?
  3. Flutter app: does one exist yet? If so, where is it, and which routes does
     it call today? That decides whether we keep temporary aliases for the old
     paths.
  4. Scale pairing: keep the fixed User-ID in the scale firmware for now, or
     build a device-pairing endpoint in this phase?
  5. Old routes and web dashboard: drop them, or keep them for a transition?
  6. Redis sync: a shared helper called by MS1 and MS3 with the trigger aligned,
     or the trigger as the only writer?

  Once you've answered, I'll adjust the plan and wait for your go-ahead before
  starting step 1.

         +old ones as hidden aliases to the same functions.

         +mplete test client for every feature (login, scan barcode, meal

         + "path user id must equal the token's user" is compatible with

         +new_user, user}`. Only the token's *internal* claims change (`s

      45 +| **Redis / dashboard** | MS3 reads macros from the DB only | A
         +rchitecture: Redis read (<10 ms). **Two writers with different 
         +payloads** already exist for `user:{id}:macros:{date}`: MS1 wri
         +tes `protein_g, carbs_g, fat_g, meals_count, latest_item`; the

       81  **Principles**
       82  1. **One schema owner:** `shared/models.py` + Alembic. MS3 sto
           ps owning tables.
       63 -2. **One identity:** the JWT `sub` is the numeric user id ever
          -ywhere; the gateway is the only place credentials are checked 
          -for the outside world, and MS3 independently enforces ownershi
          -p.                                                            
       64 -3. **One URL contract:** MS3 serves exactly the `/api/v1/...` 
          -paths the gateway exposes, so the gateway keeps proxying paths
          - unchanged.                                                   
       65 -4. **One Redis shape:** a single shared helper produces the da
          -ily-macro payload, used by MS1, MS3 and the DB trigger.       
       66 -5. **One LLM:** MS2 (local Qwen). MS3 never calls an external 
          -AI API.                                                       
       83 +2. **One identity:** the JWT `sub` is the numeric user id ever
          +ywhere. The gateway checks credentials for the outside world; 
          +MS3 independently enforces ownership.                         
       84 +3. **One URL contract, two spellings:** canonical `/api/v1/...
          +`, plus the old paths as aliases (same handlers) until the app
          + is remodelled.                                               
       85 +4. **One Redis shape:** a single shared helper builds the dail
          +y-macro payload (MS1, MS3, DB trigger).                       
       86 +5. **Local models for the scale, cloud for the web-grounded fe
          +atures:** MS1/MS2 stay local; MS3's Gemini/OFF/DDG calls go th
          +rough one client with timeouts and graceful errors.

          - h; an app refresh flow is out of scope) |

          -led directly in dev) and, when present, requires `X-User-ID` t
          -o match the token |

● I've updated docs/tasks/tasks5.md with your answers and tagged every task P0,
  P1 or P2 so we can stitch the pipeline together quickly. I haven't touched any
  code.

  What your answers changed

  1. Barcode, OCR and classify stay in MS3, and the gateway routes them there
     instead of to MS1.
  2. Gemini stays for the AI features (coach, diet plan, recipe generator,
     recipe instructions, barcode estimates and OCR), plus the DuckDuckGo and
     OpenFoodFacts scrapers. MS3 won't use Qwen or MS2 this phase. MS3 currently
     has about 7 scattered Gemini calls, and the API key travels in the URL, so
     it ends up in logs. I scoped one small central client, as P1, that handles
     the key, model name and timeouts and returns a clean "AI unavailable"
     error instead of crashing.
  3. The existing app keeps working. The handlers and response shapes move over
     unchanged, so login and register still return the same body.
  4. No scale pairing. The scale keeps sending its fixed User-ID.
  5. Old routes: see below.
  6. Redis sync uses the shared helper plus a trigger aligned to the same
     payload.

  Old routes and the web dashboard

  - Old routes: these are the paths MS3 serves today, such as /api/auth/login,
    /api/meal-logs and /recipes/top. They have no /v1, and the app calls them.
    Removing them would break the app until it's rewritten.
  - Dashboard: this is a 5,700-line web page that shipped with MS3. It's a full
    test client for every feature and isn't reachable through the gateway.
  - What I did: I kept both. MS3 serves the new /api/v1/... paths and the old
    ones from the same handlers, with a ENABLE_LEGACY_ROUTES flag so the old
    ones can be switched off later. The app then only needs its base URL changed
    to the gateway on port 8000. The dashboard is kept as a dev-only page at
    /dev-ui. It's also useful for testing: its 33 endpoints become a
    compatibility test suite that checks each one on both path styles.
  - Removed anyway: GET /users/{email}, which lets anyone look up any account by
    email, and the public POST /recipes/train-ml. The dashboard doesn't use
    either.

  Two questions

  1. The app source: it isn't on this machine (I searched). Can you put a copy,
     or at least its API layer, in the repo? Until then I'm relying on the
     dashboard, which sends the login token on every call. If the app calls any
     route without a token, enforcing auth will return 401 for it, and I'd need
     to know.
  2. Logins: the token's internal claim changes from email to user id, so
     existing logins stop working once and users sign in again. Is that fine?

  Suggested order

  1. MS3 boots on Postgres.
  2. Register and log in through the gateway on both path styles.
  3. Scan food and see it on the dashboard.
  4. Seed recipes and fast food.
  5. Run the 33-endpoint compatibility suite.

  Once you've answered those two, I'll wait for your go-ahead to start on step
  1.

      55 +async def request_id_middleware(request: Request, call_next):  
      56 +    """Reuse the gateway's X-Request-ID (or create one) and ech
         +o it back."""                                                  
      57 +    request_id = request.headers.get("x-request-id") or str(uui
         +d.uuid4())                                                     
      58 +    start = time.perf_counter()                                
      59 +    response = await call_next(request)                        
      60 +    response.headers["X-Request-ID"] = request_id              
      61 +    response.headers["X-Process-Time-ms"] = f"{(time.perf_count
         +er() - start) * 1000:.1f}"                                     
      62 +    return response

  parse. The packages MS3 needs aren't in your venv yet, so I couldn't import it
  either.

  What's in place

  - MS3 package:
    - The converted routes are in ms3_user/. Handler bodies are unchanged, apart
      from the auth and ownership edits.
    - Each route is served at its /api/v1/... path and at its old path, so the
      existing app only needs its base URL changed. Both spellings go through
      the gateway.
  - Auth:
    - The token's sub is now the numeric user ID.
    - Every route requires a login except register, login, Google sign-in,
      reset-password and /health.
    - A user can only touch their own data, whether the ID is in the path,
      query, body, form or a delete-by-ID.
    - The email-lookup route, the public training route and the old health route
      are removed.
  - Schema: MS3's new tables and columns are added to shared/models.py, plus an
    idempotent Alembic migration 002_ms3_regional. The Redis trigger now emits
    the same payload as MS1 and also fires on DELETE.
  - Redis:
    - Both MS1 and MS3 write through one shared function, shared/macro_sync.py.
      The Postgres listener now only refreshes the cache, so events aren't
      published twice.
    - New dashboard routes: /api/v1/user/daily-summary, /user/history and
      /user/targets.
  - MS1: an unknown scale User-ID now returns 404. With
    AUTO_CREATE_DEV_USER=true it creates the user and fixes the Postgres ID
    sequence.
  - Data:
    - Fast food (3,355 items, 8 chains) gets a Postgres seeder, copied from
      MS3's own parsing code.
    - MS3's diet filters match on recipes.dietary_tag inside the SQL query. Any
      recipe without that tag disappears for Vegetarian, Vegan and Jain users,
      so the seeder backfills it.
    - All seeders now read only dataset/ms3_datasets/ and fail loudly if files
      are missing.
  - Tests: new MS3 contract tests (ms3_user/tests/test_auth_contract.py) and
    gateway routing tests (gateway/tests/test_router_ms3.py). The 13 ported MS3
    test files were written for an unauthenticated API, so expect some failures
    there. I'll fix them once I see the output.

  Commands to run (in WSL)

  journey (login, scan, dashboard, manual log, delete, ownership checks, old
  paths) is in PIPELINE_2_SOFTWARE.md (docs/testing/PIPELINE_2_SOFTWARE.md).

  Things to know

  - Logins reset once, because the token claim changed. I kept the 30-day
    session (JWT_EXPIRY_HOURS=720) to match what the app expects.
  - .env changes: I added ENV=dev, ENABLE_LEGACY_ROUTES, CORS_ORIGINS,
    GEMINI_MODEL and AUTO_CREATE_DEV_USER=true, and set the JWT expiry to 720

  - Not done yet: the central Gemini client, the live user/stream endpoint, the

● The cause is in Alembic's env.py, not in the migration. It prefers the raw
  DATABASE_URL environment variable (loaded from .env), and that still says
  @postgres:5432, a Docker-only hostname. shared/db.py rewrites it to localhost,
  but env.py bypasses that rewrite. I'll fix env.py.

● The migration tool was reading the Docker-only hostname postgres from .env
  instead of using the host-side localhost address that shared/db.py computes. I
  fixed shared/alembic/env.py to use the corrected URL. It also now refuses to
  run if Postgres can't be reached, so a migration can never run against the
  SQLite fallback by accident.

● The stitched pipeline works end to end. I read every output line.

  What your run proves

  - Login: a token minted by MS3 is accepted by the gateway. The user ID arrives
    in the token and is checked again inside MS3.
  - Scan to dashboard:
    - The gateway took the scan.
    - MS1 couldn't place the frame, so Qwen did (Banana, 5.6 s on the first
      warm-up call).
    - The meal was saved as esp32_qwen_vlm.
    - MS3's daily-summary read it from Redis: 133.5 kcal, 1 meal, latest_item:
      Banana.
    - So MS1's writes and MS3's reads agree on one Redis format.
  - Delete resync: a manual Banana made it 2 meals and 238.5 kcal. Deleting it
    dropped it back to 1 meal and 133.5 kcal. This is the case the old trigger
    missed.
  - Security: no token gave 401, another user's data gave 403, a garbage token
    gave 401, and a body user ID that didn't match the caller gave 403.
  - Existing app paths: both old paths still work through the gateway:
    /api/meal-logs/today/1 and /recipes/top.
  - Fast food: all 8 chains and the Domino's veg grouping return seeded data.

  Observations from the logs

  - Redis format of the reset script: the dashboard showed latest_item: "None
    (Reset)" after the reset script. That's harmless: the script writes its own
    payload and the first real write replaces it.
  - Old test data: the history query returned 42 rows for the last 7 days,
    including the old rows from Oct 23 and Oct 24. They include negative-weight
    rows from the scale's load cell problems. They're old test data, not a bug.
    A cleanup script is task 5.5.7 if you want one.
  - Date handling: the day boundary is UTC everywhere, including MS1. For India
    this means meals logged between midnight and 5:30 am IST count toward the
    previous day. That predates this work, but worth fixing before the app goes
    live.
  - Doubled headers: the doubled date and server response headers are cosmetic,
    from the gateway proxying MS3's response.

  I've ticked 32 completed tasks in docs/tasks/tasks5.md and added the
  verification results.

● The leftovers are written. Nothing has been run on my side; I only
  syntax-checked every new or changed Python file (all 14 parse). The tests
  below are where problems will show.

  What's new

  - Central Gemini client (gemini_client.py
    (ms3_user/services/gemini_client.py)): it replaces the 7 scattered inline
    calls (coach, diet plan, recipe generator, recipe instructions, OCR, both
    barcode paths).
    - The key is sent in a header, never in the URL, so it can't leak into logs.
    - The model comes from GEMINI_MODEL, with fallbacks. Gemini 1.5 and 2.0 may
      be retired by now, so the default is gemini-2.5-flash, and the client
      skips a model the API rejects.
    - It retries on 429 and 5xx, and a short cooldown after an outage stops
      every request waiting out a 30 s timeout.
    - The AI routes keep their offline fallback replies, because the app relies
      on them. They now add "source": "gemini" | "offline".
    - generate-instructions now returns 503 when Gemini is unavailable, instead
      of 400 or 500.
  - Live dashboard stream: GET /api/v1/user/stream sends the current summary
    immediately, then a new one whenever a meal is added or deleted, from any
    source (scale or app). It sends a heartbeat every 15 s, and the gateway has
    its own 120 s rule for it.
  - Compatibility suite: test_legacy_compat.py runs the dashboard's endpoints on
    both the old and the /api/v1 paths. It checks they answer identically and
    that the create-then-delete flows work on both.
  - For the app and ops:
    - docs/API_CONTRACT.md, the single reference the app will be built against.
    - scripts/export_openapi.py, which generates the machine-readable spec.
    - The gateway's GET /health/all.
    - scripts/cleanup_test_data.py for the junk scale rows (dry-run by default).
    - The architecture route table, which now lists barcode and OCR under MS3.
  - Router split skipped, on purpose: it only moves code between files, while
    the handlers share helpers, so the risk isn't worth it now. It's recorded in
    tasks5.

  Now, if i want to test it with the app what do i do

● Your existing app only needs two things from you: the right base URL, and a
  network path from the phone to the gateway. Since the old routes are still
  served, no app code should need to change. Follow these steps in order.

  0. Pick up the new Gemini key

  .env is only read at startup. Restart MS3 (Ctrl+C, then start it again) and
  check:
  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate && GW=http://localhost:8000
  TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST $GW/api/v1/auth/login -H "Content-Type:
  application/json" -d '{"email":"dev@nutrix.dev","password":"nutrix123"}' |
  python -c "import sys,json; print(json.load(sys.stdin)['access_token'])")
  curl -s -X POST $GW/api/v1/ai/coach -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H
  "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"message":"Suggest a high-protein
  vegetarian snack"}' | python -m json.tool
  You want "source": "gemini".

  1. Services running

  Start Docker (docker compose up -d postgres redis), then MS2, MS1, MS3 and the
  gateway. The app talks only to the gateway on port 8000.

  2. Choose how the app reaches the gateway

  ┌────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │    App runs on     │        Base URL         │       Extra setup        │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │                    │                         │ none (10.0.2.2 is the    │
  │ Android emulator   │ http://10.0.2.2:8000    │ emulator's alias for     │
  │                    │                         │ your PC)                 │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Flutter web /      │                         │ none (CORS is open by    │
  │ Chrome / Windows   │ http://localhost:8000   │ default)                 │
  │ desktop            │                         │                          │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Real phone on the  │ http://<laptop Wi-Fi    │ the port forward below   │
  │ same Wi-Fi         │ IP>:8000                │                          │
  └────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  For a real phone, WSL2 hides the gateway behind a virtual network. This is the
  same one-time step as the hardware runbook. In Windows PowerShell:
  ipconfig                      # note the Wi-Fi IPv4 address, e.g.
  192.168.29.232
  wsl -d Ubuntu -- bash -c "ip -4 addr show eth0 | grep -oP
  '(?<=inet\s)\d+(\.\d+){3}'"    # note the WSL IP
  Then in PowerShell as Administrator:
  netsh interface portproxy add v4tov4 listenport=8000 listenaddress=0.0.0.0
  connectport=8000 connectaddress=<WSL_IP>
  netsh advfirewall firewall add rule name="NutriX Gateway 8000" dir=in
  action=allow protocol=TCP localport=8000
  The WSL IP changes after a reboot, so re-run the portproxy line if the phone
  stops connecting.

  Quick test before opening the app: open http://<IP>:8000/health/all in the
  phone's browser. You should see JSON with ms1_cv, ms2_llm and ms3_user
  healthy.

  3. Point the app at it

  Change only the base URL setting (wherever the app hard-codes its old MS3
  address, probably something like :8080).
  - Android over plain HTTP (not HTTPS) is blocked by default on Android 9+. Add
    android:usesCleartextTraffic="true" to the <application> tag in
    AndroidManifest.xml for testing.
  - Login with: dev@nutrix.dev / nutrix123. That's user 1, the same account the
    scale uses, so scale scans appear on that dashboard. Or register a new
    account in the app; use a normal-looking email domain, because .local is
    rejected.
  - Everyone signs in again once, because the token format changed.
  - Google sign-in:
    - Set GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID in .env to the app's client ID(s) (comma-separated
      for web, android and ios), then restart MS3.
    - If it still holds the placeholder from the template, real Google tokens
      will be rejected.

  4. Watch what the app does

  Keep the gateway terminal visible. Each request is one JSON line with the
  path, status and a request_id. To see only failures:
  # if you start the gateway like this, problems stand out
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn gateway.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8000 --reload 2>&1
  | grep --line-buffered -E '"status_code": (4|5)[0-9][0-9]'

  What each result means:

  ┌───────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  You see  │                           Meaning                           │
  ├───────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 401 on a  │ The app isn't sending the token on that call. Tell me the   │
  │ route     │ route and I'll see whether to relax it or fix the app.      │
  ├───────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 403       │ The app is sending a different user's ID in the path or     │
  │           │ body than the logged-in user.                               │
  ├───────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │           │ The app calls a route that doesn't exist. Two old ones were │
  │ 404       │  intentionally removed: GET /users/{email} and POST         │
  │           │ /recipes/train-ml.                                          │
  ├───────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 422       │ The app is sending a body shape the server rejects. Paste   │
  │           │ the JSON error.                                             │
  ├───────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 502/504   │ A backend service is down or slow.                          │
  └───────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  5. The best end-to-end demo

  With the app open on the dashboard (logged in as user 1), run a scan from your
  laptop:
  PYTHONPATH=. python scripts/dev_capture.py --image
  inference_outputs/latest_raw.jpg --weight 150 --user-id 1 \
    --url $GW/api/v1/ingest/weight-frame --token "$(grep '^DEVICE_TOKEN=' .env |
  cut -d= -f2)" --no-browser
  The meal should show up in the app's diary and the rings should update. If the
  app has no live view, pull-to-refresh will show it. A live stream
  (/api/v1/user/stream) is available when the app is ready to use it.

  Why is this still not working

● The log shows all three models returning HTTP 400. I'll check the key in .env

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Found the bug: your .env key is wrapped in quotes, and since python-dotenv
  isn't installed, the fallback loader doesn't strip them, so Google rejects the
  key and my client mistakenly cycles through models instead of surfacing the
  real error. I'll fix the loaders to strip quotes and make the client log
  Google's actual error and stop immediately on a bad key. · summary

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● That was the problem, and both parts are now fixed.

  What I changed:
  - .env loading: the loaders in shared/db.py, gateway/config.py and
    ms2_llm/config.py now strip surrounding quotes. GEMINI_API_KEY="..." and
    GEMINI_API_KEY=... behave identically from now on.
  - The Gemini client:
    - It strips quotes from the key itself as well.
    - It logs Google's actual error message, which it didn't before.
    - If Google says the key is invalid, it stops at once with that message
      instead of trying two more models.

  Restart MS3, the gateway and MS2, since all three load .env at startup, then
  check:

  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate && GW=http://localhost:8000
  PYTHONPATH=. pytest ms3_user/tests/test_gemini_client.py -q -p no:warnings
       # 2 new tests: quoted key + bad-key fail-fast

  TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST $GW/api/v1/auth/login -H "Content-Type:
  application/json" -d '{"email":"dev@nutrix.dev","password":"nutrix123"}' |
  python -c "import sys,json; print(json.load(sys.stdin)['access_token'])")
  curl -s -X POST $GW/api/v1/ai/coach -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H
  "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"message":"Suggest a high-protein
  vegetarian snack"}' | python -m json.tool

  - If it works: you'll see "source": "gemini" with a real answer.
  - If it's still offline: the MS3 log now says why, and it will be one of two
    things.
    - A line like Gemini rejected the API key: API key not valid... means the
      key itself is the problem. In that case, copy it again from Google AI
      Studio. Keys there normally start with AIza; yours is 53 characters and
      doesn't, so confirm it's a Gemini API key and not a different kind of
      credential, such as a Vertex or service-account key.
    - A line like Gemini gemini-2.5-flash rejected the request: HTTP 404 ... not
      found means that model name isn't available to your key. Set GEMINI_MODEL
      in .env to one your key lists.

  If it fails again, paste the new MS3 log lines. They now carry Google's exact
  reason.

  What can we do for fallback.

● The log explains everything, and it points at flaws in my retry logic more
  than at Google:
  - gemini-3.8-flash is a brand-new model under heavy load: 503 "high demand",
    twice, each taking about 5 s.
  - The next model (3.7-flash) then hit a ReadTimeout, because I gave every
    attempt the whole 30 s budget, so a slow model burned all of it.
  - My client also treated that timeout as "the network is down" and gave up on
    the remaining models.

  So the fallback chain never really got to run. There are two layers of fix.

  Layer 1: make the model fallback behave properly.
  - Cap each attempt at about 10 s.
  - Move straight to the next model on a 503 or a slow response.
  - Only stop on a true connection failure.
  - Order the models so the newest, most overloaded one isn't first.

  Layer 2: a real fallback behind Gemini. Today the last resort is a canned
  paragraph that ignores your question. A better chain is Gemini, then your
  local Qwen on MS2, then the canned text. That gives you real, personalised
  answers even when Google is down or overloaded, at zero cost. It needs the
  llm/generate endpoint that was deferred from Phase 4, which is small. I'll
  build both layers. Layer 1 first.

● The fallback chain is built, but not run: I only syntax-checked the new files.
  Here's the situation and what I changed.

  Why Gemini kept failing

  Your log shows two separate problems:
  - Overloaded new model: gemini-3.8-flash returned 503 "high demand" twice,
    about 5 s each.
  - My retry logic: the next model (3.7-flash) then hit a read timeout, because
    I gave every attempt the whole 30 s budget. I also treated that timeout as
    "the network is down" and gave up on the rest. The chain never reached the
    later models.

  What I changed

  - Gemini client:
    - Each attempt is now capped at 10 s (GEMINI_REQUEST_TIMEOUT).
    - A 503 or slow response moves straight to the next model, and only a true
      connection failure stops the chain.
    - The default order puts the newest, most overloaded model later: 3.7-flash,
      then 3.5-flash, 3.8-flash, 3.5-flash-lite and 3.1-flash-lite.
  - A real fallback instead of canned text: MS3 now tries Gemini first, then
    your local Qwen model on MS2, then the canned reply as a last resort.
    - MS2 gains the small POST /api/v1/llm/generate endpoint that was deferred
      from Phase 4.
    - Every AI answer is labelled source: "gemini", "local" or "offline".
    - Local answers cost nothing and work offline, but they're shorter and less
      polished than Gemini's.
    - Switch it off with AI_LOCAL_FALLBACK=false.
    - Only the text features use it. OCR and the barcode photo reader already
      have their own local fallbacks.

  Commands to run

  MS2 needs a restart for the new endpoint (about 30 s to load the model).
  Restart MS3 too.

  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate

  # 1. Tests (MS2 and MS3 tests must be separate runs)
  PYTHONPATH=. pytest ms2_llm/tests -q -p no:warnings
  PYTHONPATH=. pytest ms3_user/tests/test_gemini_client.py
  ms3_user/tests/test_ai_fallback.py -q -p no:warnings
  PYTHONPATH=. pytest ms3_user/tests -q --tb=short -p no:warnings 2>&1 | tail
  -15

  # 2. The new MS2 endpoint directly
  curl -s -X POST localhost:8003/api/v1/llm/generate -H "Content-Type:
  application/json" \
    -d '{"prompt":"Suggest a high-protein vegetarian snack in two
  lines","max_new_tokens":80}' | python -m json.tool

  # 3. Coach through the gateway: expect "gemini", or "local" if Gemini is busy
  GW=http://localhost:8000
  TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST $GW/api/v1/auth/login -H "Content-Type:
  application/json" -d '{"email":"dev@nutrix.dev","password":"nutrix123"}' |
  python -c "import sys,json; print(json.load(sys.stdin)['access_token'])")
  curl -s -X POST $GW/api/v1/ai/coach -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H
  "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"message":"Suggest a high-protein
  vegetarian snack"}' | python -m json.tool

  To force the local path and see it work, stop MS3 and start it with an empty
  key (this overrides .env):
  GEMINI_API_KEY= PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn ms3_user.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port
  8002 --reload
  Repeat the coach curl: it should answer with "source": "local", in about 10 to
  20 s.

  What to expect

  - Gemini works: the reply has "source": "gemini", and the MS3 log shows which
    model answered and any 503s before it.
  - Gemini still too slow or busy: the log shows each skipped model, then
    Answered by the local model, and the reply says "source": "local". That
    means the fallback is doing its job.
  - Local fails too: you get "source": "offline". The log lines will say why,
    most likely MS2 not running.

● The local fallback works end to end: the coach answered with "source": "local"
  after Gemini failed, and the log shows the chain moving model by model.

  Now to fixing Gemini properly. The log says what's wrong: gemini-3.7-flash and
  gemini-3.5-flash each sat silent for the full 10 s (ReadTimeout). Those
  aren't network errors, because the model-list call worked instantly. The
  models are simply slow to answer. Likely causes are:
  - the newer 3.x models "think" before answering by default, which can add many
    seconds;
  - the answer is long (your coach reply was about 400 words);
  - they're overloaded.

  I don't want to guess, so the first step is to measure. I'll write a benchmark
  that times every usable flash model with a tiny prompt and a coach-sized
  prompt, shows how many "thinking" tokens each one spent, and tests which
  config options your key accepts. I'll also add a setting so you can apply what
  it finds without code changes.

● Run the benchmark first. It makes real calls with your key, a few dozen small
  ones, and takes a minute or two.

  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate
  PYTHONPATH=. pytest ms3_user/tests/test_gemini_client.py -q -p no:warnings
   # 2 new tests for the config setting

  How to read it

  What you see: Tiny prompt is fast (under 2 s) but the coach prompt is slow,
  with a large think= number
  What it means: The model spends its time "thinking" before answering
  What to do: Use a thinking setting that the --variants section shows as
  accepted and faster, via GEMINI_GENERATION_CONFIG
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What you see: Slow only when the answer is long
  What it means: Output length
  What to do: Add maxOutputTokens, and ask for a shorter answer
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What you see: Even the tiny prompt takes more than 5 s, or returns 503 /
  ReadTimeout
  What it means: The model is overloaded or Google is slow from your network
  What to do: Skip it: pick models that answered well, or try again at a
  different time
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What you see: A model returns 404 or 400
  What it means: Retired, or not available to your key
  What to do: Don't use it

  Under the table it prints a suggested GEMINI_MODEL and GEMINI_FALLBACK_MODELS,
  ordered by actual coach-prompt speed among models that succeeded. Copy those
  into .env.

  Apply the result

  1. Put the suggested model lines in .env.
  2. If a variant like thinkingBudget=0 or thinkingLevel=low was accepted and
     noticeably faster, set it, for example:
     GEMINI_GENERATION_CONFIG={"thinkingConfig": {"thinkingLevel": "low"},
     "maxOutputTokens": 700}
  3. Restart MS3 and re-run the coach curl. You want "source": "gemini" in about
     2 to 6 s.

  If the benchmark shows Gemini is fast but MS3 still times out

  That would mean the 10 s per-attempt cap is too tight for the answer length.
  Raise GEMINI_REQUEST_TIMEOUT (for example to 20). If the benchmark shows the
  models are simply slow and reliable but not fast, the best next step is for me
  to switch the client to streaming (streamGenerateContent), which starts
  delivering text early and never times out mid-answer. I'll do that only if the
  numbers say it's needed.

  Paste the benchmark table. It's short, and it decides which of these we do.

● The benchmark answers the question clearly, and Gemini itself is fine. The
  problem was which models I put first.

  - Lite models are fast and reliable: gemini-3.5-flash-lite took 1.9 to 2.2 s,
    and gemini-3.1-flash-lite and gemini-flash-lite-latest took 2 to 4 s. They
    don't spend time "thinking".
  - The full flash models think, and that's the slowness: 3.5-flash took 10 s
    and spent 1,329 thinking tokens. 3.6-flash took 8 s, 3-flash-preview 5.4 s,
    and flash-latest 18 s.
  - The newest models are unusable right now: 3.8-flash and 3.7-flash timed out
    or returned 503 even on a one-word prompt. That's exactly what your log
    showed. The retired 2.5 models return 404.
  - Config options: thinkingBudget=0 is rejected (400), so don't use it. The
    lite models need no thinking limit at all, so no GEMINI_GENERATION_CONFIG is
    needed.

  I'll pass over the benchmark's own suggestion for one entry:
  gemini-3.1-flash-lite-preview timed out for 40 s on the tiny prompt, so it's
  flaky. Instead I'll put a thinking model last as a quality fallback.

  The barcode failure is only my curl syntax, and the error comes from curl, not

  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate && GW=http://localhost:8000
  TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST $GW/api/v1/auth/login -H "Content-Type:
  application/json" -d '{"email":"dev@nutrix.dev","password":"nutrix123"}' |

  # Barcode photo -> decode the barcode -> product lookup
  curl -s -X POST $GW/api/v1/barcode/scan-image -H "Authorization: Bearer
  $TOKEN" \
    -F "file=@inference_outputs/barcode.jpg" -F "diet_type=Vegetarian" | python
  -m json.tool | head -40

  # Nutrition-facts label photo -> extracted nutrients (a different photo)
  curl -s -X POST $GW/api/v1/ocr/upload -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
    -F "file=@inference_outputs/<a label photo>.jpg" | python -m json.tool |
  head -30

  # If you already know the number, test just the lookup chain
  curl -s "$GW/api/v1/barcode/8901058852712" -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" |
  python -m json.tool | head -25

  What the barcode path does

  1. Decode: local OpenCV tries to read the barcode first. If that fails, Gemini
     vision reads it.
  2. Look up the number, in this order, stopping at the first hit:
     - the curated Indian packaged-food list and the barcode_cache table
       (instant);
     - OpenFoodFacts;
     - a web search scraper that finds the product name;
     - a Gemini nutrition estimate;
     - the local food database.
  3. Result: a source field in the response (curated_fmcg, cache, openfoodfacts,
     gemini_ai, ...) tells you which step answered. The first lookup of a
     number is slowest. Afterwards it's cached.

  What to check in the output

  - status: success with a product name, brand, nutrients_per_100g, a health
    score and healthier alternatives means the whole chain worked.
  - If the barcode isn't read: the response says so. Check the MS3 log. A line
    mentioning generativelanguage with HTTP 200 means Gemini vision ran. Make
    sure the barcode fills a good part of the photo and is sharp.
  - If it's read but the product is unknown: the response should still return an
    estimate with a lower-confidence source. Tell me which number, and I'll
    trace which tier answered.

  Paste the scan-image output (and the MS3 log lines if it fails). If the label
  photo's numbers look wrong, I'll add a separate stronger GEMINI_VISION_MODEL
  for images, as I mentioned.
