● The package is ready to build. The AI table in the contract is also repaired.

  What to send

  Send one zip with four files, nothing else. This is the full list of what the
  developer needs:

  ┌──────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         File         │                      Purpose                      │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                      │ their starting page: backend URL, test login, a   │
  │ APP_HANDOFF.md       │ 10-minute first run, which endpoint feeds which   │
  │                      │ screen, live updates, behaviours to know about,   │
  │                      │ and what you need back from them                  │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                      │ the complete contract: auth, every endpoint with  │
  │ API_CONTRACT.md      │ its request and response shape, errors and the    │
  │                      │ live stream                                       │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ openapi_ms3.json     │ the machine-readable spec, for generating Dart    │
  │                      │ models                                            │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ nutrix_requests.http │ ready-to-send sample requests for VS Code's REST  │
  │                      │ Client, Postman or Insomnia                       │
  └──────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Do not send your repo, .env (it holds your Gemini key, JWT secret and device
  token), the datasets, the model weights, or the scale or device token. The app
  never needs any of them.

  Build it

  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate
  PYTHONPATH=. python scripts/make_app_handoff.py --base-url <the URL they will
  use, see below>
  # -> dist/nutrix_app_handoff.zip  (also copy it to Windows:  cp
  dist/nutrix_app_handoff.zip /mnt/c/Users/HP/Desktop/)
  The script regenerates the OpenAPI spec first and fills your backend URL and
  test login into the handoff page and the .http file. It runs fine without
  --base-url, but the URL then stays a placeholder.

  They also need a running backend

  Documents alone aren't enough: they need a backend they can reach. Pick one:

  1. Same Wi-Fi as your laptop (simplest if you sit together). URL is
     http://<your laptop's Wi-Fi IP>:8000, using the port-forwarding steps I
     gave earlier.
  2. A public tunnel (best if they work remotely). Run cloudflared tunnel --url
     http://localhost:8000 on your Windows host (or ngrok, which the old MS3
     used). It prints an https://... address with no router setup, and HTTPS
     also spares them the Android cleartext workaround.
     - Treat the tunnel as temporary: while it's open, anyone with the URL can
       reach your dev backend, which has open registration and no rate limiting.
       The dev account holds no sensitive data. Close the tunnel when they
       aren't testing, and change the test password if you leave it up.
  3. They run it themselves. I'd avoid this: it needs Docker, the Python
     environment, the 55 MB of git-ignored datasets and, for the scale features,
     a GPU. The app only needs the gateway, MS3, Postgres and Redis.

  Whichever you choose, keep these running while they test: docker compose up -d
  postgres redis, MS3, MS1 and MS2 (optional for them), and the gateway.

  What to ask them for

  The handoff page ends with three requests; ask them to reply with:
  1. the Google OAuth client IDs (web, Android, iOS), which you then put in .env
     as GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID (comma-separated);
  2. the list of API calls the current app makes, or the file containing them,
     especially any call that doesn't send the login token, since those will now
     get 401;
  3. each problem report with the X-Request-ID response header and the time, so
     you can find that exact request in your logs.

  Once they send the API layer, I can check every call against the contract
  myself, which is the one task still open in tasks5.

● Follow these steps in order. I'm assuming the developer is remote. If they sit
  on your Wi-Fi, use the note at the end of step 3.

  Step 1: Start everything (one terminal each, in WSL)

  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate
  docker compose up -d postgres redis
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn ms2_llm.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8003
  # terminal A (no --reload, ~30 s to load)
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn ms1_cv.main:app  --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8001 --reload
  # terminal B
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn ms3_user.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8002 --reload
  # terminal C
  PYTHONPATH=. uvicorn gateway.main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8000 --reload
  # terminal D

  Step 2: Give the test account a password you choose

  The developer will see it, so don't reuse a real one. In a fifth terminal:
  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate
  PYTHONPATH=. python scripts/create_dev_user.py --password
  'PickSomethingNew123'
  curl -s localhost:8000/health/all | python -m json.tool      # ms1, ms2, ms3
  healthy (ms4 "down" is expected)

  Step 3: Open a public address for the gateway

  In Windows PowerShell (not WSL):
  winget install Cloudflare.cloudflared        # one-time; then close and reopen
  PowerShell
  cloudflared tunnel --url http://localhost:8000
  - It prints an address like https://something-random.trycloudflare.com. Copy
    it and leave this window open. Closing it shuts the tunnel.
  - Check it from your phone's browser (on mobile data is fine):
    https://something-random.trycloudflare.com/health/all.
  - If the developer is on your Wi-Fi instead, skip the tunnel. Use http://<your
    laptop's Wi-Fi IP>:8000, after the netsh portproxy and firewall steps from
    earlier.

  Step 4: Build the zip with that address inside

  Back in WSL (use your URL and your password from step 2):
  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate
  PYTHONPATH=. python scripts/make_app_handoff.py --base-url
  https://something-random.trycloudflare.com --password 'PickSomethingNew123'
  cp dist/nutrix_app_handoff.zip /mnt/c/Users/HP/Desktop/
  You now have nutrix_app_handoff.zip on your Windows Desktop, containing only
  APP_HANDOFF.md, API_CONTRACT.md, openapi_ms3.json and nutrix_requests.http.

  Step 5: Send it

  Send that one zip (email, Drive or WhatsApp) with this message:

  ▎ Hi! This is the backend package for the NutriX app. Everything's in
  ▎ APP_HANDOFF.md; start with section 3 ("first ten minutes"). Your task: point
  ▎ the existing app's base URL at the address in that file, sign in with the
  ▎ test account, and check that the screens load. The old API paths still work,
  ▎ so you shouldn't need big code changes at first.
  ▎
  ▎ Please send me back: (1) the Google OAuth client IDs the app uses (web /
  ▎ Android / iOS), (2) the file or list showing which API calls the current app
  ▎ makes, and (3) for any problem, the X-Request-ID response header plus the
  ▎ time it happened. I'll keep the backend running while you test; tell me when
  ▎ you're starting.

  While they test

  - Keep the four terminals and the PowerShell tunnel window open.
  - If the tunnel restarts, its address changes (it's a quick tunnel). Re-run
    step 4 with the new URL and re-send the zip, or just message them the new
    address.
  - When they send the Google client IDs, put them in .env as
    GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID=id1,id2,id3, then restart MS3.
  - Close the tunnel (Ctrl+C in PowerShell) whenever they're not testing. While
    it's open, anyone with the address can reach your dev backend.

  Do not send

  .env, the repo, the datasets, the model files, or your Gemini key. The app
  never needs them.

  When they send you the app's API calls, paste them here and I'll check each
  one against the contract.

● The app developer's work plan is now in the package. The package has five

  cd /home/harsh/Nutrix && source .venv/bin/activate
  PYTHONPATH=. python scripts/make_app_handoff.py --base-url

  The zip now holds APP_HANDOFF.md, APP_BUILD_GUIDE.md (new), API_CONTRACT.md,
  openapi_ms3.json and nutrix_requests.http.

  How they update the app

  The build guide, APP_BUILD_GUIDE.md (docs/APP_BUILD_GUIDE.md), tells them the
  following:
  - Rule 1: because the backend is backward compatible, they can migrate screen
    by screen. The old paths still answer, so the app keeps working throughout.
    Only two changes are mandatory: every call now needs the login token, and
    everyone signs in once.
  - Rule 2: they build one networking layer first.
    - It's a single Dio client whose base URL comes from build config
      (--dart-define=API_BASE_URL=...), never from code.
    - It attaches the token to every call and signs the user out on a 401, but
      not for a wrong password.
    - It turns error bodies into readable messages.
    - Every screen then talks to repositories on top of it.
  - Rule 3: they follow nine stages, and each ends in something demoable. The
    stages are: connect, auth and onboarding, dashboard, logging, scanning, food
    discovery, AI, the live stream, and clean-up. Each stage has a "done when"
    check.
  - The tricky code is written out in Dart:
    - the Dio client and interceptors;
    - Google sign-in (the server needs the ID token, so Android needs the web
      client ID);
    - multipart barcode and label uploads;
    - the live-stream reader.
  - The old-to-new path table is a find-and-replace list for their existing
    code. It lists the five renames:
    - calculate-profile to user/profile;
    - recommend-foods to foods/recommend;
    - generate-meal-plan to meal-plans/generate;
    - track-weight to weight-logs;
    - ocr to ocr/upload.
      It also lists the two routes that were removed.
  - UI notes and a definition of done:
    - icons for meal sources, including how to show llm-timeout;
    - a small badge when an AI answer is local or offline;
    - drive onboarding from is_onboarded, not local flags;
    - a checklist for when the app is finished, including an Android release
      build over HTTPS.

  Send it as before

  Use the message from my last answer, plus one line: "Your task is in
  APP_BUILD_GUIDE.md; do it stage by stage, and send me the Google client IDs
  plus anything that fails, with the X-Request-ID header." The other steps,
  tunnel and keeping the terminals running, are unchanged.

  One caveat

  I wrote the Dart snippets without compiling them, and I haven't seen the app's
  actual code. They use the packages the architecture lists (Dio, Riverpod,
  flutter_secure_storage, google_sign_in, http_parser), so they should need only
  small adjustments to the app's existing structure. If they find the guide
  wrong or incomplete for their codebase, ask them to tell you where, and I'll
  fix it.
